I’m not trying to be careless or anything—it just feels unusually hard to get started and stay with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties happen more with tasks that feel boring or uninteresting to you, or does it seem to happen across the board, even with tasks you care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Mostly with the stuff that takes real sitting-down brain work, I think. It’s not that I don’t care about it—monthly reports matter, and planning a multi-site health event definitely matters—but those are the things where I feel like I just hit a wall.\n\nI’ll keep putting them off even when I know I shouldn’t. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lazy or anything… it just feels harder than it should to get started and stay with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with staying focused or starting tasks that go back to when you were a child or teenager, or is this something that’s mostly happened more recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back pretty far, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, because I did okay in school overall, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always kind of… daydreaming.\n\nI’d lose worksheets, or forget to turn in homework, and my mom basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually made it from school to home and back again. So, yes, I guess it wasn’t only an adulthood thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt unusually restless, fidgety, or as if you have a hard time sitting still for extended periods, especially when you're trying to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift around in my chair like anyone else, but I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move or like I can’t sit still.\n\nFor me it’s more… I’m sitting there, but my mind isn’t cooperating, if that makes sense. But physically restless? I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these issues with staying focused or starting tasks also happen in other parts of your life, like at home, in conversations, or during leisure activities, or is it mostly limited to work or school-related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work, no. I mean, work is where it shows up the most because there are deadlines and other people notice, but it kind of spills over.\n\nAt home and with friends, I’m still functioning, I think—I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—but I’ve definitely had people cover for me or remind me about things. Deadlines have slipped, and I’ve had to send those embarrassing last-minute “I’m so sorry, I’m still working on it” messages to coworkers. Even with friends, I’ve had to apologize for dropping the ball on something I said I’d do.\n\nSo… I’m getting by, but not as cleanly as I probably make it look. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I can think of a few. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.

I mean, I have normal bad days where I feel kind of discouraged or drained, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nThe hopeless part… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your daily life—outside of work—where your difficulty with focus or follow-through has caused problems, like missing appointments, forgetting things, or not finishing tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of a few. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.\n\nI help out with some community and event stuff, and I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers more than once. Usually I’ll remember later and try to smooth it over, but it’s still not great. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been forgetting to return calls from volunteers, missing small errands for events, and once or twice I’ve had to apologize because a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.\n\nI help out with some community and event stuff, and I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers more than once. Usually I’ll remember later and try to smooth it over, but it’s still not great. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\nPatient: I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. That’s happened with volunteers a few times, and I’ve felt bad because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care.\n\nWith errands, especially small ones, I’ll agree to pick something up or follow up on something, and then later realize I didn’t do it. Usually I try to fix it quickly, but it can turn into scrambling.\n\nAnd once or twice I’ve had a meeting completely slip my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” That was embarrassing, and I apologized. I guess in my personal routine it’s that kind of thing—little responsibilities slipping unless I’m really on top of reminders. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you describe how often you need external reminders or prompts from others to stay on top of tasks, and does this happen in different areas of your life—like at work, at home, and with friends—or mostly in just one area?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been forgetting to return calls from volunteers, missing small errands for events, and once or twice I’ve had to apologize because a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say I rely on reminders more than I probably want to admit. Not every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable.

It happens more with the community/event stuff, honestly. Like I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers, or I’ll miss a small errand I said I’d handle, and then I’m trying to catch up after the fact. And once or twice a meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming, which was… embarrassing.

At home or with friends, it’s maybe less obvious, or I’ve built little workarounds, but I do better when someone texts me or there’s some kind of prompt. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yeah, reminders from other people definitely help me stay on track. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

It’s not just one setting, no. I wish it were, because then I could just say, “Okay, it’s only when I’m busy with events” or something.

I’m still getting by, I think — work gets done, community things mostly happen — but there are gaps. At work, deadlines have slipped, or someone else has had to step in and cover something I didn’t follow through on as quickly as I should have. At home or with friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to respond or I’ll have to apologize at the last minute because I dropped the ball.

I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m functioning, but it does show up across different parts of my life. And honestly, the embarrassing part is having to do those “I’m sorry, I completely missed that” conversations with coworkers or friends. Does that make sense?

I wish it were, because then I could just say, “Okay, it’s only when I’m busy with events” or something.\n\nI’m still getting by, I think — work gets done, community things mostly happen — but there are gaps. At home or with friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to respond or I’ll have to apologize at the last minute because I dropped the ball.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m functioning, but it does show up across different parts of my life. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different parts of your day, like at work, at home, or when you're out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah, it’s across different parts of the day, not just one situation. I mean, I’m still getting by — I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart — but there are definitely gaps.

I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lazy or anything… it just feels harder than it should to get started and stay with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with staying focused or starting tasks that go back to when you were a child or teenager, or is this something that’s mostly happened more recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back pretty far, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, because I did okay in school overall, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always kind of… daydreaming.\n\nI’d lose worksheets, or forget to turn in homework, and my mom basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually made it from school to home and back again. So, yes, I guess it wasn’t only an adulthood thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt unusually restless, fidgety, or as if you have a hard time sitting still for extended periods, especially when you're trying to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift around in my chair like anyone else, but I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move or like I can’t sit still.\n\nFor me it’s more… I’m sitting there, but my mind isn’t cooperating, if that makes sense. But physically restless? I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these issues with staying focused or starting tasks also happen in other parts of your life, like at home, in conversations, or during leisure activities, or is it mostly limited to work or school-related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work, no. I mean, work is where it shows up the most because there are deadlines and other people notice, but it kind of spills over.\n\nAt home and with friends, I’m still functioning, I think—I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—but I’ve definitely had people cover for me or remind me about things. Deadlines have slipped, and I’ve had to send those embarrassing last-minute “I’m so sorry, I’m still working on it” messages to coworkers. Even with friends, I’ve had to apologize for dropping the ball on something I said I’d do.\n\nSo… I’m getting by, but not as cleanly as I probably make it look. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting more frustrated, discouraged, or down on yourself because of these difficulties, or do you feel like your mood is generally holding steady despite the challenges?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially when I know something shouldn’t be that hard and I still put it off or miss a detail.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’m, like, seriously down all the time or anything. My mood is mostly okay, I think. It’s more embarrassment or irritation with myself in the moment. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, separate from feeling frustrated about these focus issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal bad days where I feel kind of discouraged or drained, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nThe hopeless part… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your daily life—outside of work—where your difficulty with focus or follow-through has caused problems, like missing appointments, forgetting things, or not finishing tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of a few. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\nPatient: I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. That’s happened with volunteers a few times, and I’ve felt bad because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care.\n\nWith errands, especially small ones, I’ll agree to pick something up or follow up on something, and then later realize I didn’t do it. Usually I try to fix it quickly, but it can turn into scrambling.\n\nAnd once or twice I’ve had a meeting completely slip my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” That was embarrassing, and I apologized. I mean, I have normal bad days where I feel kind of discouraged or drained, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nThe hopeless part… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. It’s more like I get annoyed with myself or disappointed when I’m not keeping up the way I should. But separate from that, I think my mood is generally okay. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your daily life—outside of work—where your difficulty with focus or follow-through has caused problems, like missing appointments, forgetting things, or not finishing tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of a few. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.\n\nI help out with some community and event stuff, and I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers more than once. Usually I’ll remember later and try to smooth it over, but it’s still not great. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\nPatient: I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. That’s happened with volunteers a few times, and I’ve felt bad because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care.\n\nWith errands, especially small ones, I’ll agree to pick something up or follow up on something, and then later realize I didn’t do it. Usually I try to fix it quickly, but it can turn into scrambling.\n\nAnd once or twice I’ve had a meeting completely slip my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” That was embarrassing, and I apologized. I guess in my personal routine it’s that kind of thing—little responsibilities slipping unless I’m really on top of reminders. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you need external reminders or prompts from others to stay on top of tasks, and does this happen in different areas of your life—like at work, at home, and with friends—or mostly in just one area?\n\nPatient: I’d say I rely on reminders more than I probably want to admit. Not every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nIt happens more with the community/event stuff, honestly. Like I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers, or I’ll miss a small errand I said I’d handle, and then I’m trying to catch up after the fact. And once or twice a meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming, which was… embarrassing.\n\nAt home or with friends, it’s maybe less obvious, or I’ve built little workarounds, but I do better when someone texts me or there’s some kind of prompt. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yeah, reminders from other people definitely help me stay on track. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different settings throughout your day, like at work, at home, or when you’re out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one setting, no. I wish it were, because then I could just say, “Okay, it’s only when I’m busy with events” or something.\n\nI’m still getting by, I think — work gets done, community things mostly happen — but there are gaps. At work, deadlines have slipped, or someone else has had to step in and cover something I didn’t follow through on as quickly as I should have. At home or with friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to respond or I’ll have to apologize at the last minute because I dropped the ball.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m functioning, but it does show up across different parts of my life. And honestly, the embarrassing part is having to do those “I’m sorry, I completely missed that” conversations with coworkers or friends. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different parts of your day, like at work, at home, or when you're out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s across different parts of the day, not just one situation. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties happen more with tasks that feel boring or uninteresting to you, or does it seem to happen across the board, even with tasks you care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Mostly with the stuff that takes real sitting-down brain work, I think. It’s not that I don’t care about it—monthly reports matter, and planning a multi-site health event definitely matters—but those are the things where I feel like I just hit a wall.\n\nI’ll keep putting them off even when I know I shouldn’t. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lazy or anything… it just feels harder than it should to get started and stay with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with staying focused or starting tasks that go back to when you were a child or teenager, or is this something that’s mostly happened more recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back pretty far, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, because I did okay in school overall, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always kind of… daydreaming.\n\nI’d lose worksheets, or forget to turn in homework, and my mom basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually made it from school to home and back again. So, yes, I guess it wasn’t only an adulthood thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt unusually restless, fidgety, or as if you have a hard time sitting still for extended periods, especially when you're trying to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift around in my chair like anyone else, but I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move or like I can’t sit still.\n\nFor me it’s more… I’m sitting there, but my mind isn’t cooperating, if that makes sense. But physically restless? I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these issues with staying focused or starting tasks also happen in other parts of your life, like at home, in conversations, or during leisure activities, or is it mostly limited to work or school-related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work, no. I mean, work is where it shows up the most because there are deadlines and other people notice, but it kind of spills over.\n\nAt home and with friends, I’m still functioning, I think—I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—but I’ve definitely had people cover for me or remind me about things. Deadlines have slipped, and I’ve had to send those embarrassing last-minute “I’m so sorry, I’m still working on it” messages to coworkers. Even with friends, I’ve had to apologize for dropping the ball on something I said I’d do.\n\nSo… I’m getting by, but not as cleanly as I probably make it look. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting more frustrated, discouraged, or down on yourself because of these difficulties, or do you feel like your mood is generally holding steady despite the challenges?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially when I know something shouldn’t be that hard and I still put it off or miss a detail.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’m, like, seriously down all the time or anything. My mood is mostly okay, I think. It’s more embarrassment or irritation with myself in the moment. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, separate from feeling frustrated about these focus issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal bad days where I feel kind of discouraged or drained, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nThe hopeless part… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. It’s more like I get annoyed with myself or disappointed when I’m not keeping up the way I should. But separate from that, I think my mood is generally okay. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your daily life—outside of work—where your difficulty with focus or follow-through has caused problems, like missing appointments, forgetting things, or not finishing tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of a few. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.\n\nI help out with some community and event stuff, and I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers more than once. Usually I’ll remember later and try to smooth it over, but it’s still not great. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\nPatient: I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. That’s happened with volunteers a few times, and I’ve felt bad because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care.\n\nWith errands, especially small ones, I’ll agree to pick something up or follow up on something, and then later realize I didn’t do it. Usually I try to fix it quickly, but it can turn into scrambling.\n\nAnd once or twice I’ve had a meeting completely slip my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” That was embarrassing, and I apologized. I guess in my personal routine it’s that kind of thing—little responsibilities slipping unless I’m really on top of reminders. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you need external reminders or prompts from others to stay on top of tasks, and does this happen in different areas of your life—like at work, at home, and with friends—or mostly in just one area?\n\nPatient: I’d say I rely on reminders more than I probably want to admit. Not every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nIt happens more with the community/event stuff, honestly. Like I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers, or I’ll miss a small errand I said I’d handle, and then I’m trying to catch up after the fact. And once or twice a meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming, which was… embarrassing.\n\nAt home or with friends, it’s maybe less obvious, or I’ve built little workarounds, but I do better when someone texts me or there’s some kind of prompt. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yeah, reminders from other people definitely help me stay on track. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different settings throughout your day, like at work, at home, or when you’re out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one setting, no. I wish it were, because then I could just say, “Okay, it’s only when I’m busy with events” or something.\n\nI’m still getting by, I think — work gets done, community things mostly happen — but there are gaps. At work, deadlines have slipped, or someone else has had to step in and cover something I didn’t follow through on as quickly as I should have. At home or with friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to respond or I’ll have to apologize at the last minute because I dropped the ball.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m functioning, but it does show up across different parts of my life. And honestly, the embarrassing part is having to do those “I’m sorry, I completely missed that” conversations with coworkers or friends. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different parts of your day, like at work, at home, or when you're out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s across different parts of the day, not just one situation. I mean, I’m still getting by — I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart — but there are definitely gaps.\n\nAt work and with community responsibilities, deadlines have slipped, or someone’s had to jump in and cover something I didn’t quite follow through on. And then with friends or coworkers, I’ve had those awkward last-minute apologies where I’m saying, “I’m sorry, I missed that,” or “I meant to get back to you.”\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I try to keep it together, and most of the time I do, but it does show up in more than one area. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the issues you’ve noticed with focus or follow-through happen in different types of situations — like at work, at home, or during social activities — or if they mostly occur in just one kind of setting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely more than one setting. I’m still getting by, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not handling my life or anything, but there are places where it shows.\n\nAt work and with community stuff, people have had to cover gaps for me sometimes, or deadlines have slipped because I didn’t stay on top of something the way I should have. And socially, or with coworkers, I’ve had to do those awkward last-minute apologies — like, “I’m sorry, I missed that,” or “I meant to follow up.”\n\nIt’s embarrassing to admit, but it’s not just one kind of situation. It kind of follows me around a bit, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting impulsively without thinking things through, or feeling a strong need to be constantly doing something, even if it’s not necessarily physical movement?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as impulsive, at least not in a big obvious way. I don’t think I’m doing risky things or anything like that.\n\nThe “constantly doing something” part… maybe? I do tend to feel like I should be productive or handling something, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s more mental than physical, I guess. Like I’m not bouncing around, but I can feel kind of driven to keep moving through tasks or thinking about what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nBut I don’t know if that really counts. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do think a lot, and sometimes I’m kind of running through things in my head, but I don’t know that I’d call it restlessness exactly.

I don’t want to overstate it, because I did okay in school overall, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always kind of… daydreaming.\n\nI’d lose worksheets, or forget to turn in homework, and my mom basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually made it from school to home and back again. Like, if I need to sit down and do monthly reports or plan out a larger multi-site health event, it’s like I hit a wall.\n\nI usually do get things done eventually, but I put them off more than I should, even when I know they’re important. I’m not trying to be careless or anything—it just feels unusually hard to get started and stay with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties happen more with tasks that feel boring or uninteresting to you, or does it seem to happen across the board, even with tasks you care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Mostly with the stuff that takes real sitting-down brain work, I think. It’s not that I don’t care about it—monthly reports matter, and planning a multi-site health event definitely matters—but those are the things where I feel like I just hit a wall.\n\nI’ll keep putting them off even when I know I shouldn’t. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lazy or anything… it just feels harder than it should to get started and stay with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with staying focused or starting tasks that go back to when you were a child or teenager, or is this something that’s mostly happened more recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back pretty far, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, because I did okay in school overall, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always kind of… daydreaming.\n\nI’d lose worksheets, or forget to turn in homework, and my mom basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually made it from school to home and back again. So, yes, I guess it wasn’t only an adulthood thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt unusually restless, fidgety, or as if you have a hard time sitting still for extended periods, especially when you're trying to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift around in my chair like anyone else, but I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move or like I can’t sit still.\n\nFor me it’s more… I’m sitting there, but my mind isn’t cooperating, if that makes sense. But physically restless? I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these issues with staying focused or starting tasks also happen in other parts of your life, like at home, in conversations, or during leisure activities, or is it mostly limited to work or school-related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work, no. I mean, work is where it shows up the most because there are deadlines and other people notice, but it kind of spills over.\n\nAt home and with friends, I’m still functioning, I think—I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—but I’ve definitely had people cover for me or remind me about things. Deadlines have slipped, and I’ve had to send those embarrassing last-minute “I’m so sorry, I’m still working on it” messages to coworkers. Even with friends, I’ve had to apologize for dropping the ball on something I said I’d do.\n\nSo… I’m getting by, but not as cleanly as I probably make it look. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting more frustrated, discouraged, or down on yourself because of these difficulties, or do you feel like your mood is generally holding steady despite the challenges?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially when I know something shouldn’t be that hard and I still put it off or miss a detail.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’m, like, seriously down all the time or anything. My mood is mostly okay, I think. It’s more embarrassment or irritation with myself in the moment. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, separate from feeling frustrated about these focus issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal bad days where I feel kind of discouraged or drained, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nThe hopeless part… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. It’s more like I get annoyed with myself or disappointed when I’m not keeping up the way I should. But separate from that, I think my mood is generally okay. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your daily life—outside of work—where your difficulty with focus or follow-through has caused problems, like missing appointments, forgetting things, or not finishing tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of a few. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.\n\nI help out with some community and event stuff, and I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers more than once. Usually I’ll remember later and try to smooth it over, but it’s still not great. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\nPatient: I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. That’s happened with volunteers a few times, and I’ve felt bad because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care.\n\nWith errands, especially small ones, I’ll agree to pick something up or follow up on something, and then later realize I didn’t do it. Usually I try to fix it quickly, but it can turn into scrambling.\n\nAnd once or twice I’ve had a meeting completely slip my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” That was embarrassing, and I apologized. I guess in my personal routine it’s that kind of thing—little responsibilities slipping unless I’m really on top of reminders. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you need external reminders or prompts from others to stay on top of tasks, and does this happen in different areas of your life—like at work, at home, and with friends—or mostly in just one area?\n\nPatient: I’d say I rely on reminders more than I probably want to admit. Not every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nIt happens more with the community/event stuff, honestly. Like I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers, or I’ll miss a small errand I said I’d handle, and then I’m trying to catch up after the fact. And once or twice a meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming, which was… embarrassing.\n\nAt home or with friends, it’s maybe less obvious, or I’ve built little workarounds, but I do better when someone texts me or there’s some kind of prompt. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yeah, reminders from other people definitely help me stay on track. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different settings throughout your day, like at work, at home, or when you’re out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one setting, no. I wish it were, because then I could just say, “Okay, it’s only when I’m busy with events” or something.\n\nI’m still getting by, I think — work gets done, community things mostly happen — but there are gaps. At work, deadlines have slipped, or someone else has had to step in and cover something I didn’t follow through on as quickly as I should have. At home or with friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to respond or I’ll have to apologize at the last minute because I dropped the ball.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m functioning, but it does show up across different parts of my life. And honestly, the embarrassing part is having to do those “I’m sorry, I completely missed that” conversations with coworkers or friends. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different parts of your day, like at work, at home, or when you're out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s across different parts of the day, not just one situation. I mean, I’m still getting by — I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart — but there are definitely gaps.\n\nAt work and with community responsibilities, deadlines have slipped, or someone’s had to jump in and cover something I didn’t quite follow through on. And then with friends or coworkers, I’ve had those awkward last-minute apologies where I’m saying, “I’m sorry, I missed that,” or “I meant to get back to you.”\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I try to keep it together, and most of the time I do, but it does show up in more than one area. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the issues you’ve noticed with focus or follow-through happen in different types of situations — like at work, at home, or during social activities — or if they mostly occur in just one kind of setting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely more than one setting. I’m still getting by, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not handling my life or anything, but there are places where it shows.\n\nAt work and with community stuff, people have had to cover gaps for me sometimes, or deadlines have slipped because I didn’t stay on top of something the way I should have. And socially, or with coworkers, I’ve had to do those awkward last-minute apologies — like, “I’m sorry, I missed that,” or “I meant to follow up.”\n\nIt’s embarrassing to admit, but it’s not just one kind of situation. It kind of follows me around a bit, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting impulsively without thinking things through, or feeling a strong need to be constantly doing something, even if it’s not necessarily physical movement?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as impulsive, at least not in a big obvious way. I don’t think I’m doing risky things or anything like that.\n\nThe “constantly doing something” part… maybe? I do tend to feel like I should be productive or handling something, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s more mental than physical, I guess. Like I’m not bouncing around, but I can feel kind of driven to keep moving through tasks or thinking about what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nBut I don’t know if that really counts. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are times when you feel an internal sense of restlessness, like your mind is constantly going, even if you’re not physically moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do think a lot, and sometimes I’m kind of running through things in my head, but I don’t know that I’d call it restlessness exactly.\n\nI’m usually sitting still fine, at least on the outside. Internally, maybe there are times my mind feels busy, but I guess I’ve always thought of that as just normal stress or trying to keep up with things. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIs that the kind of internal restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your difficulties with focus or follow-through were noticeable in childhood settings such as school, or have they become more apparent only as an adult in work or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think it was there when I was a kid, yeah. I mean, I did okay overall, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always daydreaming or not keeping track of things.\n\nI remember losing worksheets, and homework was a thing — not always because I hadn’t done it, but because I’d forget to turn it in or it would be buried somewhere. My mom used to check my backpack pretty much every night to make sure things actually made it back to school.\n\nSo I guess it wasn’t only adult work responsibilities. It’s just become harder to compensate for now, maybe. Is that the kind of childhood pattern you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are times when you feel an internal sense of urgency or mental restlessness, like your brain is going a mile a minute, even if you're not physically moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to that exactly. I mean, I do have times where my head feels busy, but “a mile a minute” sounds stronger than what I’d usually say.\n\nIt’s more like I’m thinking through what I need to do or trying to keep myself on track, not so much an urgent feeling all the time. I don’t know if I’m describing that right. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing unless I’m specifically paying attention to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "",

[
  {

I don’t want to overstate it, because I did okay in school overall, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always kind of… daydreaming.\n\nI’d lose worksheets, or forget to turn in homework, and my mom basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually made it from school to home and back again. So, yes, I guess it wasn’t only an adulthood thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt unusually restless, fidgety, or as if you have a hard time sitting still for extended periods, especially when you're trying to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift around in my chair like anyone else, but I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move or like I can’t sit still.\n\nFor me it’s more… I’m sitting there, but my mind isn’t cooperating, if that makes sense. But physically restless? I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these issues with staying focused or starting tasks also happen in other parts of your life, like at home, in conversations, or during leisure activities, or is it mostly limited to work or school-related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work, no. I mean, work is where it shows up the most because there are deadlines and other people notice, but it kind of spills over.\n\nAt home and with friends, I’m still functioning, I think—I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—but I’ve definitely had people cover for me or remind me about things. Deadlines have slipped, and I’ve had to send those embarrassing last-minute “I’m so sorry, I’m still working on it” messages to coworkers. Even with friends, I’ve had to apologize for dropping the ball on something I said I’d do.\n\nSo… I’m getting by, but not as cleanly as I probably make it look. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting more frustrated, discouraged, or down on yourself because of these difficulties, or do you feel like your mood is generally holding steady despite the challenges?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially when I know something shouldn’t be that hard and I still put it off or miss a detail.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’m, like, seriously down all the time or anything. My mood is mostly okay, I think. It’s more embarrassment or irritation with myself in the moment. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, separate from feeling frustrated about these focus issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal bad days where I feel kind of discouraged or drained, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nThe hopeless part… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your daily life—outside of work—where your difficulty with focus or follow-through has caused problems, like missing appointments, forgetting things, or not finishing tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of a few. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.\n\nI help out with some community and event stuff, and I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers more than once. Usually I’ll remember later and try to smooth it over, but it’s still not great. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\nPatient: I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. That’s happened with volunteers a few times, and I’ve felt bad because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care.\n\nWith errands, especially small ones, I’ll agree to pick something up or follow up on something, and then later realize I didn’t do it. Usually I try to fix it quickly, but it can turn into scrambling.\n\nAnd once or twice I’ve had a meeting completely slip my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” That was embarrassing, and I apologized. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you need external reminders or prompts from others to stay on top of tasks, and does this happen in different areas of your life—like at work, at home, and with friends—or mostly in just one area?\n\nPatient: I’d say I rely on reminders more than I probably want to admit. I mean, I might shift around in my chair like anyone else, but I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move or like I can’t sit still.\n\nFor me it’s more… I’m sitting there, but my mind isn’t cooperating, if that makes sense. But physically restless? I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these issues with staying focused or starting tasks also happen in other parts of your life, like at home, in conversations, or during leisure activities, or is it mostly limited to work or school-related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work, no. I mean, work is where it shows up the most because there are deadlines and other people notice, but it kind of spills over.\n\nAt home and with friends, I’m still functioning, I think—I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is—but I’ve definitely had people cover for me or remind me about things. Deadlines have slipped, and I’ve had to send those embarrassing last-minute “I’m so sorry, I’m still working on it” messages to coworkers. Even with friends, I’ve had to apologize for dropping the ball on something I said I’d do.\n\nSo… I’m getting by, but not as cleanly as I probably make it look. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting more frustrated, discouraged, or down on yourself because of these difficulties, or do you feel like your mood is generally holding steady despite the challenges?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially when I know something shouldn’t be that hard and I still put it off or miss a detail.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’m, like, seriously down all the time or anything. My mood is mostly okay, I think. It’s more embarrassment or irritation with myself in the moment. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, separate from feeling frustrated about these focus issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal bad days where I feel kind of discouraged or drained, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nThe hopeless part… no, I wouldn’t really describe it that way. It’s more like I get annoyed with myself or disappointed when I’m not keeping up the way I should. But separate from that, I think my mood is generally okay. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your daily life—outside of work—where your difficulty with focus or follow-through has caused problems, like missing appointments, forgetting things, or not finishing tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of a few. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is, but it does happen.\n\nI help out with some community and event stuff, and I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers more than once. Usually I’ll remember later and try to smooth it over, but it’s still not great. I’ve also missed small errands I was supposed to do for events—like something simple that I agreed to pick up or follow up on—and then I’m scrambling.\n\nAnd, uh… once or twice, a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming. That was embarrassing. I apologized, and people were nice about it, but I felt pretty bad. Is that the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulty with focus and follow-through impacts your daily routine, especially in settings outside of work and community events—like managing personal responsibilities, household tasks, or interactions with family?\n\nPatient: I mean, at home it’s probably not as dramatic as it sounds, but it does show up. I’ll mean to call someone back and then it just sort of falls out of my head unless something reminds me. That’s happened with volunteers a few times, and I’ve felt bad because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care.\n\nWith errands, especially small ones, I’ll agree to pick something up or follow up on something, and then later realize I didn’t do it. Usually I try to fix it quickly, but it can turn into scrambling.\n\nAnd once or twice I’ve had a meeting completely slip my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” That was embarrassing, and I apologized. I guess in my personal routine it’s that kind of thing—little responsibilities slipping unless I’m really on top of reminders. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you need external reminders or prompts from others to stay on top of tasks, and does this happen in different areas of your life—like at work, at home, and with friends—or mostly in just one area?\n\nPatient: I’d say I rely on reminders more than I probably want to admit. Not every single day, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nIt happens more with the community/event stuff, honestly. Like I’ve forgotten to return calls from volunteers, or I’ll miss a small errand I said I’d handle, and then I’m trying to catch up after the fact. And once or twice a meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me asking if I was coming, which was… embarrassing.\n\nAt home or with friends, it’s maybe less obvious, or I’ve built little workarounds, but I do better when someone texts me or there’s some kind of prompt. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yeah, reminders from other people definitely help me stay on track. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different settings throughout your day, like at work, at home, or when you’re out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one setting, no. I wish it were, because then I could just say, “Okay, it’s only when I’m busy with events” or something.\n\nI’m still getting by, I think — work gets done, community things mostly happen — but there are gaps. At work, deadlines have slipped, or someone else has had to step in and cover something I didn’t follow through on as quickly as I should have. At home or with friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to respond or I’ll have to apologize at the last minute because I dropped the ball.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m functioning, but it does show up across different parts of my life. And honestly, the embarrassing part is having to do those “I’m sorry, I completely missed that” conversations with coworkers or friends. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you’ve noticed these issues with focus or follow-through happening in different parts of your day, like at work, at home, or when you're out with friends — or do they mostly show up in just one kind of situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s across different parts of the day, not just one situation. I mean, I’m still getting by — I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart — but there are definitely gaps.\n\nAt work and with community responsibilities, deadlines have slipped, or someone’s had to jump in and cover something I didn’t quite follow through on. And then with friends or coworkers, I’ve had those awkward last-minute apologies where I’m saying, “I’m sorry, I missed that,” or “I meant to get back to you.”\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I try to keep it together, and most of the time I do, but it does show up in more than one area. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the issues you’ve noticed with focus or follow-through happen in different types of situations — like at work, at home, or during social activities — or if they mostly occur in just one kind of setting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely more than one setting. I’m still getting by, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not handling my life or anything, but there are places where it shows.\n\nAt work and with community stuff, people have had to cover gaps for me sometimes, or deadlines have slipped because I didn’t stay on top of something the way I should have. And socially, or with coworkers, I’ve had to do those awkward last-minute apologies — like, “I’m sorry, I missed that,” or “I meant to follow up.”\n\nIt’s embarrassing to admit, but it’s not just one kind of situation. It kind of follows me around a bit, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting impulsively without thinking things through, or feeling a strong need to be constantly doing something, even if it’s not necessarily physical movement?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as impulsive, at least not in a big obvious way. I don’t think I’m doing risky things or anything like that.\n\nThe “constantly doing something” part… maybe? I do tend to feel like I should be productive or handling something, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s more mental than physical, I guess. Like I’m not bouncing around, but I can feel kind of driven to keep moving through tasks or thinking about what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nBut I don’t know if that really counts. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are times when you feel an internal sense of restlessness, like your mind is constantly going, even if you’re not physically moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do think a lot, and sometimes I’m kind of running through things in my head, but I don’t know that I’d call it restlessness exactly.\n\nI’m usually sitting still fine, at least on the outside. Internally, maybe there are times my mind feels busy, but I guess I’ve always thought of that as just normal stress or trying to keep up with things. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIs that the kind of internal restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your difficulties with focus or follow-through were noticeable in childhood settings such as school, or have they become more apparent only as an adult in work or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think it was there when I was a kid, yeah. I mean, I did okay overall, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but even in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but that I was always daydreaming or not keeping track of things.\n\nI remember losing worksheets, and homework was a thing — not always because I hadn’t done it, but because I’d forget to turn it in or it would be buried somewhere. My mom used to check my backpack pretty much every night to make sure things actually made it back to school.\n\nSo I guess it wasn’t only adult work responsibilities. It’s just become harder to compensate for now, maybe. Is that the kind of childhood pattern you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are times when you feel an internal sense of urgency or mental restlessness, like your brain is going a mile a minute, even if you're not physically moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to that exactly. I mean, I do have times where my head feels busy, but “a mile a minute” sounds stronger than what I’d usually say.\n\nIt’s more like I’m thinking through what I need to do or trying to keep myself on track, not so much an urgent feeling all the time. I don’t know if I’m describing that right. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing unless I’m specifically paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you experience these focus or follow-through issues even in situations where you're highly interested or motivated, or do they mostly happen when you're dealing with less engaging tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worse with the boring or routine stuff, but… honestly, it can happen even when I care about it.\n\nThe main thing that made me come in is that anything that takes real sitting-down brain work — like monthly reports, or planning a multi-site health event — I kind of hit a wall. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you experience these focus or follow-through issues even in situations where you're highly interested or motivated, or do they mostly happen when you're dealing with less engaging tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I’d say it’s not only when I don’t care.
